I work in a warehouse, and I do inventory counts, moving around stock, checking numbers, that kind of thing—and lately it’s like every little thing pulls me off track. A beeper goes off, or a forklift backs up and the alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet nearby, and I’ll look over for just a second, but then I’ve lost the count. Then I go back and think, “Was I at 24 or 34?” and sometimes I don’t even realize I messed it up until later, when the inventory numbers are wrong.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve done this kind of work a long time, and I’m used to noise and movement around me. Warehouses aren’t quiet places, you know? But this feels different. It’s like my attention just gets yanked away before I can stop it, and then I’m entering the wrong numbers or having to redo things. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless at work or in other situations?\n\nPatient: Restless… yeah, I guess that fits, but for me it’s more like I keep wanting to do things, if that makes sense. Like at work, instead of just doing my regular inventory and stock stuff, I’ve been taking on these extra safety-improvement projects. I’ll notice something like a walkway sign that could be clearer, or a shelf area that could be organized better, and then I’m making notes, talking to people, trying to set up a whole plan around it.\n\nAnd outside of work, too, I’ve been signing up for more art workshops than I normally would. Usually I might do one here or there, but lately I’m looking at calendars and making lists and schedules like I’m running some kind of whole operation. It’s not exactly just tapping my foot or squirming in a chair—though I probably do some of that too—it’s more like I can’t leave things alone. I keep adding projects and activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, and it’s kind of hard to admit because I don’t usually think of myself as reckless. But lately I’ve been doing things where, afterward, I look back and think, “Why didn’t I just slow down for ten minutes and think about whether that made sense?”\n\nThe main thing is spending. I’ve gone to a few craft markets and ended up buying way more than I should have—paints, frames, little tools, supplies for projects. At the time it all seemed completely reasonable, like, “Oh, I’ll use this for that idea,” or “This could turn into something really useful,” and I’d have this whole picture in my head of what I was going to make with it. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me. I know that may sound like a small thing, but I’ve done this kind of work long enough that I’m used to being dependable. So when I have to go back and ask someone to verify what I did, or admit I made an error, it makes me feel embarrassed and unsure of myself. It kind of follows me home too, because I keep thinking about it.\n\nAnd then at home, my husband being retired was supposed to be this adjustment where we’d settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, like we’re both trying not to set the other one off. That’s not how I wanted this time to feel. So yes, overall, I’d say it has lowered my quality of life some. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel as comfortable or steady in my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: Did these changes in your behavior start right after your husband's retirement or was there a delay?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much right around when he retired, yes. I don’t think it was months later or anything like that. It feels like it started after he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.\n\nI’d say it’s been about a month now. At first I thought maybe it was just the adjustment, because suddenly he was home during the day and the rhythm of the house was different, and I was trying to get used to that. But looking back, that’s when I started noticing these changes more clearly. So I’d put it at about a month, tied pretty closely to his retirement and the change in our usual routine.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, not six months. It’s been much shorter than that—about a month, I’d say.\n\nIt really seemed to start around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our usual routine changed. That’s the point I keep coming back to, because before that, things felt more familiar and predictable. Once he was home more and the rhythm of the house shifted, that’s when I started noticing all of this. So, no, I wouldn’t say six months—more like about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past where you had similar difficulties with attention and impulse control that lasted less than six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’m trying to think back, and I can’t immediately point to another specific period where it was like this in the same way.\n\nI mean, everybody has times where they’re distracted or they say something too quickly and regret it, so I don’t want to act like I’ve never had moments like that. But if you mean a noticeable stretch where attention and impulse control were enough of a problem that I was making more mistakes or feeling out of step with myself, I don’t know that I can identify one clearly.\n\nNothing is coming to mind right now that I’d say was a definite similar episode. It’s possible I’m forgetting something, but this current situation feels more noticeable to me because it’s been tied to what’s been going on recently at home and in my routine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Bipolar I Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it A beeper goes off, or a forklift backs up and the alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet nearby, and I’ll look over for just a second, but then I’ve lost the count. Then I go back and think, “Was I at 24 or 34?” and sometimes I don’t even realize I messed it up until later, when the inventory numbers are wrong.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve done this kind of work a long time, and I’m used to noise and movement around me. I’ll notice something like a walkway sign that could be clearer, or a shelf area that could be organized better, and then I’m making notes, talking to people, trying to set up a whole plan around it.\n\nAnd outside of work, too, I’ve been signing up for more art workshops than I normally would. Usually I might do one here or there, but lately I’m looking at calendars and making lists and schedules like I’m running some kind of whole operation. I’ve gone to a few craft markets and ended up buying way more than I should have—paints, frames, little tools, supplies for projects. At the time it all seemed completely reasonable, like, “Oh, I’ll use this for that idea,” or “This could turn into something really useful,” and I’d have this whole picture in my head of what I was going to make with it. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me. I know that may sound like a small thing, but I’ve done this kind of work long enough that I’m used to being dependable. So when I have to go back and ask someone to verify what I did, or admit I made an error, it makes me feel embarrassed and unsure of myself. It kind of follows me home too, because I keep thinking about it.\n\nAnd then at home, my husband being retired was supposed to be this adjustment where we’d settle into a new routine together. It’s possible I’m forgetting something, but this current situation feels more noticeable to me because it’s been tied to what’s been going on recently at home and in my routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods in your life where you had symptoms like these for less than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me. I know that may sound like a small thing, but I’ve done this kind of work long enough that I’m used to being dependable. So when I have to go back and ask someone to verify what I did, or admit I made an error, it makes me feel embarrassed and unsure of myself. It kind of follows me home too, because I keep thinking about it.\n\nAnd then at home, my husband being retired was supposed to be this adjustment where we’d settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, like we’re both trying not to set the other one off. That’s not how I wanted this time to feel. So yes, overall, I’d say it has lowered my quality of life some. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel as comfortable or steady in my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: Did these changes in your behavior start right after your husband's retirement or was there a delay?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much right around when he retired, yes. I don’t think it was months later or anything like that. It feels like it started after he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.\n\nI’d say it’s been about a month now. At first I thought maybe it was just the adjustment, because suddenly he was home during the day and the rhythm of the house was different, and I was trying to get used to that. But looking back, that’s when I started noticing these changes more clearly. So I’d put it at about a month, tied pretty closely to his retirement and the change in our usual routine.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, not six months. It’s been much shorter than that—about a month, I’d say.\n\nIt really seemed to start around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our usual routine changed. That’s the point I keep coming back to, because before that, things felt more familiar and predictable. Once he was home more and the rhythm of the house shifted, that’s when I started noticing all of this. So, no, I wouldn’t say six months—more like about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past where you had similar difficulties with attention and impulse control that lasted less than six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’m trying to think back, and I can’t immediately point to another specific period where it was like this in the same way.\n\nI mean, everybody has times where they’re distracted or they say something too quickly and regret it, so I don’t want to act like I’ve never had moments like that. But if you mean a noticeable stretch where attention and impulse control were enough of a problem that I was making more mistakes or feeling out of step with myself, I don’t know that I can identify one clearly.\n\nNothing is coming to mind right now that I’d say was a definite similar episode. It’s possible I’m forgetting something, but this current situation feels more noticeable to me because it’s been tied to what’s been going on recently at home and in my routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods in your life where you had symptoms like these for less than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to sort of scan back through my life, and nothing stands out clearly to me as another period like this.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful times, sure, and times where I probably wasn’t as focused as I should’ve been, or I was more scattered than usual. But I don’t know that I could honestly say, “Yes, there was this other stretch that lasted a few weeks or a couple months and it was the same kind of thing.” I’m not remembering anything specific enough to say that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not that I can identify. If there was something, it’s not coming to mind in a clear way right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms like these before, even if they didn't last as long as the ones you're having now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really say yes to that. I keep thinking maybe there were little moments here and there in the past where I wasn’t as focused or I acted before thinking, but nothing that stands out to me as a real “episode,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s hard because once you ask the question, I start second-guessing myself—like, was that normal stress, or was that something similar? But I can’t point to a specific time and say, “Yes, that happened before.” Not clearly. This feels more noticeable to me now, but before this, I don’t remember having the same kind of problem in a way that I would’ve labeled it or paid much attention to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I keep adding projects and activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, and it’s kind of hard to admit because I don’t usually think of myself as reckless. But lately I’ve been doing things where, afterward, I look back and think, “Why didn’t I just slow down for ten minutes and think about whether that made sense?”\n\nThe main thing is spending. I’ve gone to a few craft markets and ended up buying way more than I should have—paints, frames, little tools, supplies for projects. At the time it all seemed completely reasonable, like, “Oh, I’ll use this for that idea,” or “This could turn into something really useful,” and I’d have this whole picture in my head of what I was going to make with it. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me. I know that may sound like a small thing, but I’ve done this kind of work long enough that I’m used to being dependable. So when I have to go back and ask someone to verify what I did, or admit I made an error, it makes me feel embarrassed and unsure of myself. Six months or longer… no, I don’t think I can say that. I’ve been trying to think back, and if you mean a clear period where this same kind of thing was going on for half a year or more, I’m not aware of anything like that.

It’s not exactly just tapping my foot or squirming in a chair—though I probably do some of that too—it’s more like I can’t leave things alone. I keep adding projects and activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, and it’s kind of hard to admit because I don’t usually think of myself as reckless. But lately I’ve been doing things where, afterward, I look back and think, “Why didn’t I just slow down for ten minutes and think about whether that made sense?”\n\nThe main thing is spending. I’ve gone to a few craft markets and ended up buying way more than I should have—paints, frames, little tools, supplies for projects. At the time it all seemed completely reasonable, like, “Oh, I’ll use this for that idea,” or “This could turn into something really useful,” and I’d have this whole picture in my head of what I was going to make with it. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me. I know that may sound like a small thing, but I’ve done this kind of work long enough that I’m used to being dependable. So when I have to go back and ask someone to verify what I did, or admit I made an error, it makes me feel embarrassed and unsure of myself. It kind of follows me home too, because I keep thinking about it.\n\nAnd then at home, my husband being retired was supposed to be this adjustment where we’d settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, like we’re both trying not to set the other one off. That’s not how I wanted this time to feel. So yes, overall, I’d say it has lowered my quality of life some. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel as comfortable or steady in my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: Did these changes in your behavior start right after your husband's retirement or was there a delay?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much right around when he retired, yes. I don’t think it was months later or anything like that. It feels like it started after he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.\n\nI’d say it’s been about a month now. At first I thought maybe it was just the adjustment, because suddenly he was home during the day and the rhythm of the house was different, and I was trying to get used to that. But looking back, that’s when I started noticing these changes more clearly. So I’d put it at about a month, tied pretty closely to his retirement and the change in our usual routine.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, not six months. It’s been much shorter than that—about a month, I’d say.\n\nIt really seemed to start around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our usual routine changed. That’s the point I keep coming back to, because before that, things felt more familiar and predictable. Once he was home more and the rhythm of the house shifted, that’s when I started noticing all of this. So, no, I wouldn’t say six months—more like about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past where you had similar difficulties with attention and impulse control that lasted less than six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’m trying to think back, and I can’t immediately point to another specific period where it was like this in the same way.\n\nI mean, everybody has times where they’re distracted or they say something too quickly and regret it, so I don’t want to act like I’ve never had moments like that. But if you mean a noticeable stretch where attention and impulse control were enough of a problem that I was making more mistakes or feeling out of step with myself, I don’t know that I can identify one clearly.\n\nNothing is coming to mind right now that I’d say was a definite similar episode. It’s possible I’m forgetting something, but this current situation feels more noticeable to me because it’s been tied to what’s been going on recently at home and in my routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods in your life where you had symptoms like these for less than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to sort of scan back through my life, and nothing stands out clearly to me as another period like this.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful times, sure, and times where I probably wasn’t as focused as I should’ve been, or I was more scattered than usual. But I don’t know that I could honestly say, “Yes, there was this other stretch that lasted a few weeks or a couple months and it was the same kind of thing.” I’m not remembering anything specific enough to say that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not that I can identify. If there was something, it’s not coming to mind in a clear way right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms like these before, even if they didn't last as long as the ones you're having now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really say yes to that. I keep thinking maybe there were little moments here and there in the past where I wasn’t as focused or I acted before thinking, but nothing that stands out to me as a real “episode,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s hard because once you ask the question, I start second-guessing myself—like, was that normal stress, or was that something similar? But I can’t point to a specific time and say, “Yes, that happened before.” Not clearly. This feels more noticeable to me now, but before this, I don’t remember having the same kind of problem in a way that I would’ve labeled it or paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes like this in the past that lasted less than six months but then went away on their own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to answer that honestly, and I’m not coming up with a clear example where it happened before for a shorter stretch and then just kind of resolved on its own.\n\nThere have been times in life where I’ve been under pressure or not as organized as I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d call those the same thing. I don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had normal rough patches, if that makes sense. Nothing really stands out as an episode where I thought, “Oh, this is happening,” and then it passed.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is: not that I can remember or identify. If there was something, it wasn’t obvious enough to stick in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where symptoms like these lasted for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Six months or longer… no, I don’t think I can say that. I’ve been trying to think back, and if you mean a clear period where this same kind of thing was going on for half a year or more, I’m not aware of anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had long stretches in life that were stressful or busy, and sometimes when you’re working and dealing with day-to-day stuff, everything kind of blurs together. So part of me wonders if I would even notice it clearly looking back. But nothing comes to mind where I’d say, “Yes, that was happening for six months,” or anything that definite.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no—not that I remember.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s possible I’m forgetting something, but this current situation feels more noticeable to me because it’s been tied to what’s been going on recently at home and in my routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other periods in your life where you had symptoms like these for less than six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve been trying to sort of scan back through my life, and nothing stands out clearly to me as another period like this.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful times, sure, and times where I probably wasn’t as focused as I should’ve been, or I was more scattered than usual. But I don’t know that I could honestly say, “Yes, there was this other stretch that lasted a few weeks or a couple months and it was the same kind of thing.” I’m not remembering anything specific enough to say that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not that I can identify. If there was something, it’s not coming to mind in a clear way right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms like these before, even if they didn't last as long as the ones you're having now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really say yes to that. I keep thinking maybe there were little moments here and there in the past where I wasn’t as focused or I acted before thinking, but nothing that stands out to me as a real “episode,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s hard because once you ask the question, I start second-guessing myself—like, was that normal stress, or was that something similar? But I can’t point to a specific time and say, “Yes, that happened before.” Not clearly. This feels more noticeable to me now, but before this, I don’t remember having the same kind of problem in a way that I would’ve labeled it or paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes like this in the past that lasted less than six months but then went away on their own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to answer that honestly, and I’m not coming up with a clear example where it happened before for a shorter stretch and then just kind of resolved on its own.\n\nThere have been times in life where I’ve been under pressure or not as organized as I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d call those the same thing. I don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had normal rough patches, if that makes sense. Nothing really stands out as an episode where I thought, “Oh, this is happening,” and then it passed.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is: not that I can remember or identify. If there was something, it wasn’t obvious enough to stick in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where symptoms like these lasted for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Six months or longer… no, I don’t think I can say that. I’ve been trying to think back, and if you mean a clear period where this same kind of thing was going on for half a year or more, I’m not aware of anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had long stretches in life that were stressful or busy, and sometimes when you’re working and dealing with day-to-day stuff, everything kind of blurs together. So part of me wonders if I would even notice it clearly looking back. But nothing comes to mind where I’d say, “Yes, that was happening for six months,” or anything that definite.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no—not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms like these that lasted less than six months in the past, but then completely went away?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it a little differently, but I still don’t think I can give you a clear yes. I’m trying to think of a time where I had these same kinds of symptoms, for a few weeks or months maybe, and then they completely went away—and nothing really comes to mind in a way I’d feel confident saying was that.\n\nI’ve definitely had periods where life was hard or I was more scattered or stressed, but it’s hard for me to separate normal life stuff from what you’re asking about. I don’t remember a distinct period, like, “Oh, that happened for three months and then stopped.” If something like that did happen, it wasn’t clear enough at the time for me to recognize it or remember it now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

It was more gradual, I think. I mean, I can sort of point to when it began, but it didn’t feel like one day everything was normal and the next day it wasn’t.
