Like at work, instead of just doing my regular inventory and stock stuff, I’ve been taking on these extra safety-improvement projects. I’ll notice something like a walkway sign that could be clearer, or a shelf area that could be organized better, and then I’m making notes, talking to people, trying to set up a whole plan around it.\n\nAnd outside of work, too, I’ve been signing up for more art workshops than I normally would. Usually I might do one here or there, but lately I’m looking at calendars and making lists and schedules like I’m running some kind of whole operation. It’s not exactly just tapping my foot or squirming in a chair—though I probably do some of that too—it’s more like I can’t leave things alone. I keep adding projects and activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, and it’s kind of hard to admit because I don’t usually think of myself as reckless. But lately I’ve been doing things where, afterward, I look back and think, “Why didn’t I just slow down for ten minutes and think about whether that made sense?”\n\nThe main thing is spending. I’ve gone to a few craft markets and ended up buying way more than I should have—paints, frames, little tools, supplies for projects. At the time it all seemed completely reasonable, like, “Oh, I’ll use this for that idea,” or “This could turn into something really useful,” and I’d have this whole picture in my head of what I was going to make with it. It didn’t feel impulsive in the moment, it felt like I was being productive or prepared.\n\nBut then the credit card bill comes, and I’m just sitting there embarrassed because some of it is still in bags, or stacked up waiting for these projects I was so sure I’d get to. And it’s not like I needed all of it right then. I just bought it because it felt important in the moment, and now I’m looking at the total and feeling pretty foolish.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to get along with your coworkers or having problems at home because of these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m getting into fights with coworkers exactly, but it has started causing problems. At work, the inventory mistakes have gotten to the point where I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, and that’s humiliating because I know how to do my job. I don’t want to be the person who needs someone looking over her shoulder, especially after doing warehouse work for so long.\n\nAnd at home… yes, that’s been harder too. My husband just retired, and I think we were supposed to be settling into this new phase, you know, figuring out our routines and enjoying having more time around each other. But instead we’ve been arguing more than usual. Not huge blowups every day, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other, being careful, instead of just being comfortable in the same house. It makes me sad, honestly, because I don’t want things to feel tense at home on top of feeling like I’m messing up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… apparently I do. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but my husband has pointed it out a few times, and some coworkers too. They’ll say I’m talking really fast, like I’m trying to get everything out all at once, and they can’t really jump in.\n\nIt happens with silly things too, not just important stuff. Like I’ll start telling my husband about a recipe I want to try, and then I’m explaining the ingredients, and what I’d change, and how I could make it healthier, and then somehow I’m on to meal planning for the whole week before he’s even had a chance to say whether he likes the first idea. Or at work, if I get going about one of those safety ideas, I can hear myself listing all the steps and who we should talk to and what signs should go where, and I know I’m going on, but it feels like if I don’t finish the whole thought right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSame with the art classes—I’ll be telling someone about one workshop, and then I’m already talking about another one and what supplies I’d need. People kind of smile and wait, or they’ll say, “Slow down,” and then I feel embarrassed because I didn’t mean to take over the conversation. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get every part of the idea out before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these changes in your behavior are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is interfering, maybe not in a way where everything has completely fallen apart, but enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have been the clearest thing. I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after making errors, and that feels awful because I know how to do that job. I’ve done that kind of work a long time, so needing someone else to help me make sure I didn’t mess it up makes me feel embarrassed, like I’m not as reliable as I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affecting things too. My husband just retired, and I keep thinking this should be a nice time for us to settle into a new routine together. Instead, we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, kind of being careful about what we say. It’s not constant screaming or anything like that, but it’s tense, and that makes it harder to just enjoy being at home with him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I’d say. Not years or anything like that—at least not like this. It really seemed to start after my husband stopped working outside the home, when he retired and our regular routine kind of got turned upside down.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I had normal stressful days like anybody does, but this trouble staying on track and blurting things out or acting before I’ve really thought it through… that feels more recent. Since he’s been home more, everything in the house feels different—when we eat, when I do things, how much space we each have—and I think that’s around when I started noticing I wasn’t handling things the same way.\n\nSo, roughly a month. Maybe a little give or take, but that’s the time frame that makes sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these changes in your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Maybe “overwhelmed” is the right word, because it’s not just one thing happening in one place. It’s like I’ll tell myself, okay, I can manage this, it’s just a rough patch, but then something happens at work or at home and I realize I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, the inventory mistakes have really gotten to me. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making errors, and that’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. I don’t like feeling like someone has to look over my shoulder or that I can’t trust myself with things I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd then at home, with my husband being retired now, I wanted this to be a nice adjustment for us, like we’d have more time together and settle into a comfortable routine. But instead we’ve been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other, being careful, instead of enjoying it. That part makes me sad, honestly. So yes, it is distressing. Not like everything is ruined, but enough that I feel worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your trouble with attention and impulse control?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what’s been happening. I wouldn’t say I’m missing big deadlines every day or anything like that, but completing things the way I’m supposed to has gotten harder, especially the inventory counts.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll start counting or entering numbers, and then something pulls me off it—a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and it’s like my mind just jumps over there. Then I come back to what I was doing and I’m not completely sure where I left off, or I think I know, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because it’s not complicated in a way I don’t understand. I know how to do the work. But lately it feels like every little sound or movement interrupts me, and once I’m pulled away, I don’t always get back on track correctly. So yes, it’s making tasks take longer, and I’ve had to go back and fix things or double-check them, which makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members outside of your husband due to your recent behavior?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—or at least I haven’t noticed anything clear like that. Most of what I’ve been aware of is between me and my husband, just because he’s the one I’m around the most right now and the house routine has changed so much.\n\nWith other family or friends, I can’t say anyone has brought anything up to me. I haven’t had someone say, “You’re acting different,” or anything like that. I mean, I might be a little more distracted in general, but I don’t know that it’s affected those relationships in a way I could point to. I’d have to think about it more, but off the top of my head, no, nothing major outside of my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your recent behavior changes have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s affected my quality of life, maybe not in some dramatic way where everything has fallen apart, but enough that my day-to-day feels harder and more tense than it used to.\n\nAt work, having to double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes has really bothered me.
